The corollary for the closing round: **the filtered batch must include the test class of every previous round whose session note named the shape you are fixing** — a batch of corpus letters plus your own new class will not catch it, because the stale pin is in neither. **REINFORCED 2026-09-08: THREE such pins were inverted in FOUR consecutive rounds ((P18.44), (P18.48), (P18.49)), and the last set was created by the IMMEDIATELY PRECEDING round — a round that records its own residue as a pin hands the next round a failing suite.** The cost is not the edit, it is that a red pin asserting a known-wrong value is indistinguishable from a regression until someone re-derives it against pristine. If a residue must be visible, put the divergence in the test's KDoc and pin only what is CORRECT today; if a pin genuinely must assert today's wrong answer, its name must say so (`residue - …`) so the next round recognises the countdown on sight.
- **Test ordering sensitivity**: Adding new checker code that increases diagnostics can cause deterministic failures in unrelated JS emit tests — tests pass individually but fail in the full suite. Always run the full suite to verify no regressions.

### Kotlin idioms

- **A `when (value) { null, SOME_OBJECT, false -> … }` COMPILES TO A CHAIN OF `Intrinsics.areEqual` CALLS, AND IT IS INVISIBLE IN THE SOURCE — `jsTruthy`'s five-case `when` was **5.0% of a benchmark's samples**, spent asking whether a value equals `false` (2026-08-21).** A `when { value == null -> …; value is Boolean -> … }` form decides the same cases for a type test each. Only a leaf profile names it; the equality `when` reads as the tidier of the two.
- **NEVER key a HashMap/HashSet by an AST NODE with a large subtree (FunctionDeclaration, class/namespace bodies) — AST nodes are Kotlin data classes, so `hashCode()`/`equals()` DEEP-recurse the whole subtree on every lookup** (round 471: a `HashMap<FunctionDeclaration, String>` in buildNestedFunctionMap cost the services self-compile +10 s, 39 → 51 s; replaced by cluster-order-aligned file lists). The existing `builtinLibDecls`/`builtinLibMemberDecls` node-sets survive because lib decl subtrees are small — not a licence. Key by `Symbol.id`/`Type.id`/pos-based keys, or keep parallel lists aligned to an existing order.

- **Kotlin's `mutableMapOf()`/`mutableSetOf()` return LinkedHashMap/LinkedHashSet — ordered, so they pay `LinkedHashMap.afterNodeInsertion` on every put and an ordered copy on construction (round 483: ~5% of the compiler self-compile self-time was `afterNodeInsertion`).** On a HOT, order-INDEPENDENT map/set — especially one COPIED per scope entry — write `HashMap()`/`HashSet()` (and `HashMap(other)`/`HashSet(other)` for copies, NOT `.toMutableMap()`/`.toMutableSet()`, which always return the linked variant). The per-function-body scope structures (`currentLocalTypes`, `currentLocalDeclTypeNodes`, `currentShadowedNames`, `currentParamBindingNames`) and `getUnionType`'s dedup set are HashMap/HashSet for exactly this reason. **Before converting, PROVE order is unused**: grep the field for `.keys/.values/.entries/.forEach/.map/.iterator/.first/.joinToString/.sorted` — a map whose iteration feeds DISPLAY or DIAGNOSTIC output (member order, union display) MUST stay ordered, so this is a per-field audit, not a blanket sweep.
- **The build is WARNING-CLEAN (0 compiler warnings; the 2026-07-04 sweep cleared 1,025) — KEEP IT THAT WAY: fix every new compiler `w:` the moment it appears, never let them accumulate.** `extraWarnings = true` + `progressiveMode = true` (build.gradle.kts) mean some warnings become HARD ERRORS on a Kotlin bump (any "will become an error in language version 2.4" note), and a warning routinely flags a REAL BUG — the sweep found `minArgumentCount` was always 0 because `it.questionToken == null` compares a `Boolean` (never null). Check with `./gradlew compileKotlinJvm compileTestKotlinJvm --rerun-tasks 2>&1 | grep -a '^w:'` (needs `--rerun-tasks`; Gradle caches by content hash and does NOT re-emit warnings on an up-to-date compile). Every fix must be behavior-preserving — gate on the full corpus suite; `USELESS_CAST`/redundant-`!!`/redundant-`?.` are identical static types (safe), but "always true/false" and unused-var removals can drop a load-bearing smart-cast or side effect (a bare `x as T` statement smart-casts `x`), so compile + suite after each batch.
- **`*/` inside KDoc terminates the comment**: A `/** ... */` doc comment ends at the first `*/`. Embedding `*/` (e.g. when documenting a JSDoc pattern) silently breaks the rest of the declaration — a data-class body following a broken doc comment surfaces as a cascade of confusing errors like "Modifier 'override' is not applicable to 'local variable'", "No parameter with name 'pos' found", "Unresolved reference 'jsDocTagEnd'". Reword to "comment-close" or escape as `*\/`.
- **Kotlin block comments NEST — a literal `/**` *inside* a KDoc opens a nested comment level (B151)**: writing the JSDoc string `/** @overload ... */` inside a `/** ... */` doc comment makes the inner `/**` open a NESTED block comment, so the doc comment's closing `*/` only drops back to depth 1 and the comment runs to EOF → `Syntax error: Unclosed comment` at the last line + a cascade of "Unresolved reference" errors for class members BEFORE the doc comment (the whole class fails to parse). This is DISTINCT from the `*/`-closes-early gotcha above: here it's the `/**` opener that bites. When documenting JSDoc tag syntax in a KDoc, never write a literal `/**` (or `/*`) — describe it as "a JSDoc comment" or use single-line `//` comments. **WORSE, SILENT variant (round 390, build.gradle.kts): a path GLOB in a KDoc (e.g. writing src-slash-lib-slash-star-dot-d-dot-ts) also contains the nested-comment opener — and when a LATER declaration's `*/` happens to re-balance the depth, the file COMPILES with a silently-DEAD region: tasks/vals declared inside it simply don't exist ("Task not found", top-level statements never run), and even a syntax error appended inside the swallowed span "builds successfully". If a build-script region mysteriously does not execute, scan comment-nesting depth before anything else.**
- **No non-stdlib dependencies in `commonMain`**: The project targets Kotlin Native (in addition to JVM/JS), so `commonMain` must use only `kotlin.*` and `kotlinx.*` packages. No `java.*`, no `BigDecimal`, no JVM-only types. Use Kotlin's built-in numeric types and stdlib math (`kotlin.math.*`).
- **Enum context resolution** (Kotlin 2.1+): When the expected type is an enum, use unqualified entry names — write `Equals`, not `SyntaxKind.Equals`. This applies to `when` branch conditions, named arguments (`operator = Equals`), comparisons (`flags == VarKeyword`), and any other position where the enum type is inferred. Caveat: if a data class has the same name as an enum entry (e.g. `LabeledStatement`), keep the `SyntaxKind.` prefix to avoid ambiguity.
- **No JVM-only APIs in `commonMain`**: `Map.putIfAbsent` → use `getOrPut`; `Math.pow` → use `kotlin.math.pow` extension; `RegexOption.DOT_MATCHES_ALL` → use `[\s\S]` in the pattern (B259). `./gradlew compileKotlinJvm` does NOT catch these — it compiles only the JVM target. Always use Kotlin stdlib equivalents for multiplatform compatibility.

## AI agent mission


**PHASE 18 — TYPESCRIPT FOR THE JVM AND KOTLIN (owner directive 2026-09-01, RE-POINTS the project; supersedes the round-716 "single-thread performance" mission and, for planning purposes, the whole (INC.\*) latency arc).**

**Why.** The JetBrains WebStorm evaluation (Aug 2026) paused. Their need was a post-hoc TYPE ORACLE — the query shape of tsgo's `tsc/internal/api/proto.go` (142 methods: `getTypeAtLocation`, `getSymbolAtLocation`, Symbol/Type/Signature handles, `getPropertiesOfType`, `isTypeAssignableTo`, `getResolvedSignature`, `getContextualType`, …) — and **this checker cannot serve one**: its answers are functions of walk-scoped state, as `CheckedProgram.kt` and `TypeCapture.kt` already document. tsgo (TypeScript 7) is the free, official default; competing with it on "a TypeScript compiler" is not the mission.

**The mission.** xtsc is **TypeScript for the JVM and Kotlin**: (1) type-check and compile TypeScript with **no Node and no Go anywhere in the toolchain**; (2) a whole-program checker **embeddable in a Kotlin application** (the `Project` API); (3) a **Kotlin externals generator with resolved types** — the gap Dukat and Karakum never closed; (4) a **JVM bytecode backend** (KIR); (5) an **LSP** so anyone can try it in five minutes. tsgo cannot go there; that is the differentiation.

**What this closes.** The **(INC.\*) incremental-latency family is CLOSED**. The narrowed per-keystroke floor is 94-110 ms ((INC.72b)/(INC.89)) and the plugin's own query is 93-217 ms ((INC.90)) — no outcome above changes at that scale. **REFUSE a further (INC.\*) round unless a plugin-facing query is measured > 300 ms warm**, and do not touch `Checker.kt` for latency without that measurement in hand.

**OWNER DIRECTIVE 2026-09-12 — TYPESCRIPT 7.0 IS THE ONLY COMPATIBILITY TARGET.** "If you are also checking TypeScript 6.0 compatibility, we don't have to provide it at all. TypeScript 7.0 compatibility is enough, so if there is any legacy code supporting deprecated features, it can be removed." Consequences: (1) the reference is `tools/tsgo-7.0.2/lib/tsc` ALONE — pristine `typescript@6.0.3` is no longer an adjudicator, and where the two split (round 938's families: TS2300 at both duplicate declarations, `The last overload gave the following error.`, union member order) tsgo's answer is the one to implement; a pristine-shaped corpus baseline that then differs in FORM is switched off through `LogicalParityDivergence` (the ONLY mechanism), with the divergence recorded as "tsgo-follows"; (2) legacy code supporting features tsgo 7 REMOVES may be deleted — the authoritative list is `typescript-go-repo/internal/compiler/program.go` (`createRemovedOptionDiagnostic`, "Removed in TS7"): `baseUrl`, `outFile`, `target ES5` (and below), `module AMD`/`System`/`UMD`, `moduleResolution Classic`/`node10`, `alwaysStrict false`, `esModuleInterop false`, `allowSyntheticDefaultImports false`, `downlevelIteration` — `strictBindCallApply: false` is NOT on it; the removal arc is queued as (LEGACY.1) and is corpus-gated (those configurations are already skipped by `usesUnsupportedOption`). (3) **APPROVED 2026-09-12 (owner: "Green light to tsgo regenerated baseline"): the CORPUS is re-pinned to tsgo's REGENERATED baselines** — the `tsgo-port` branch behind tsgo 7.0.2's `_submodules/TypeScript` — as (LEGACY.0), ahead of the removal arc; this supersedes the `cloneTypeScriptRepo` entry's "never pin to the tsgo submodule sha" (that rule was the byte-parity-against-pristine era). **MEASURED THE SAME DAY (read-only sizing, `docs/legacy-removal-census.md` § 9): the `tsgo-port` branch's baselines are NOT tsgo's regenerated output — they are pristine `main` plus ONE flag flip (`stableTypeOrdering` defaulting ON, two source lines), a 791-file delta that is 658 `.types` + 72 `.errors.txt` + 35 `.js`, ZERO deleted baselines, ZERO changed cases, and exactly ONE round-938 family (union member ORDER); TS2300-at-both-declarations, TS2717 and `The last overload gave…` stay PRISTINE-shaped there. tsgo's ACTUAL output lives in `typescript-go-repo/testdata/baselines/reference/{submodule,submoduleAccepted,submoduleTriaged}/` as `.errors.txt.diff`/`.js.diff` LAYERS over the submodule baselines — 378 active subtests against the pin's 29.** So (LEGACY.0) is two pins: (0a) the `tsgo-port` sha, 29 red max (22 files one mechanical display-order family), landed alone; (0b) tsgo's own testdata layers, which needs the generator to apply `.diff` files and its own owner approval. Until (0b) lands, a tsgo-following change in those families still routes a moved baseline through `LogicalParityDivergence`.

**OWNER DIRECTIVE 2026-09-21 — COMPATIBILITY IS AN INVARIANT ON THE *SHARED* SURFACE, DIVERGENCE IS THE *EXTENSION* SURFACE; NEITHER IS A PHASE.** Three rules, so no round "improves" on tsgo by accident. (1) **The VS-Code test for any proposed SEMANTIC divergence: would code that checks clean in VS Code fail here, or vice versa? If yes, it is refused regardless of the argument.** TypeScript has no spec — tsgo IS the definition, every user's reference is their editor's hover, and every Phase-18 product (externals with resolved types, KIR, the type oracle) is only worth anything if its types are THE TypeScript types. The backend's temptation is the one to watch: TypeScript's unsoundness is answered by a STRICTER INTERNAL lowering analysis (sound subset → typed fields, the rest → the dynamic bag), never by changing a check verdict. Divergence that is legitimate and already practised: FORM (through `LogicalParityDivergence`, the only mechanism), and BUDGETS on pathological inputs (depth/computation limits that answer identically on every realistic program). (2) **A tsgo defect tsgo itself records is NOT a compatibility target: for a baseline whose `.diff` layer lives under `submoduleTriaged/` ("known diffs that we intend to fix"), the target is the INTENDED answer — the submodule baseline the layer diverges from — never tsgo's current output.** `docs/tsgo-baselines.md` § 7 already counts following a triaged bug as a regression; this makes it the rule. `submoduleAccepted/` is the opposite (a divergence tsgo has adopted) and IS the target. (3) **Extensions do not wait for the alignment tail**: the `Project` API, the oracle, externals, KIR and the LSP are a strict SUPERSET — every tsgo-valid program checks identically, xtsc additionally answers questions tsgo does not take — and anything stricter than tsgo (JVM-flavoured checks) is opt-in or an LSP hint, default = tsgo. The alignment stop-condition is the REAL-LIBRARY probe (cronstrue / marked / knip agreeing with tsgo, `docs/kir-library-readiness.md`), not corpus completeness; a divergence we find that is in NEITHER `submoduleTriaged.txt` NOR `submoduleAccepted.txt` is an upstream-reportable tsgo finding and goes in `docs/upstream-findings.md` as an ISSUE candidate (bulk test PRs are refused: our pins are Kotlin, 2,847 of them assert a SILENCE a baseline cannot express, and the conformance corpus lives in `microsoft/TypeScript`, not the Go repo).

**Pre-approved Guardrails (owner, 2026-09-01)** — these do NOT need a new user decision: two new Gradle modules, `xemantic-typescript-compiler-externals` (JVM first) and `xemantic-typescript-compiler-lsp` (JVM, distributed as a GraalVM native image through the EXISTING `nativeImage` configuration; a Kotlin/Native target is a later item). **NO new dependencies** — the LSP speaks JSON-RPC 2.0 over stdio using `kotlinx-serialization-json` + `kotlinx-io` from the version catalog; **lsp4j is NOT approved** (JVM-only Java; this repo is KMP). The README repositioning (DOC.2) lands on branch `docs/reposition`, NOT main — the owner reviews positioning text; everything else lands on main per protocol.

**Phase 17 history (kept for context).** *Self-compile the TypeScript compiler, then performance* (owner directive 2026-07-03, re-scoping 2026-07-02's "any TypeScript project"): fully compile the TypeScript compiler ITSELF, then optimize performance — the v1 exit was all 8 tsc-source profiles at zero FPs, all files emitted, zero crashes. It LANDED at round 481 and was re-verified at round 679. Pipeline: Scanner → Parser → **Binder → Checker → Transformer → Emitter**. The corpus suite (Phase 16, 100% green) is a permanent zero-regression GATE, not the goal. Live counts in `STATUS.md`. The M3 engine items' accumulated analysis lives in `docs/history/PLAN-PHASE-4.md` § "Known architectural blockers". **M5 was re-scoped 2026-07-13 (owner) to the staged architecture-inversion arc (INV.0–INV.7): read `docs/ARCHITECTURE-RETHINK.md` before ANY INV work** — kotlinx-coroutines-core in commonMain is owner-approved. Under Phase 18 the INV arc is REVIVED with a new purpose: the inversion is what would let xtsc answer a post-hoc type oracle, and `docs/INVERSION-DESIGN.md` is its design artifact.

### Execution protocol


PLAN-PHASE-5.md contains the **live QUEUE** under the heading `## Phase 17 — Self-compile the TypeScript compiler (M0–M5)` (kept verbatim for stability) → `### WORK ORDER (owner directive 2026-09-01) — PHASE 18: TypeScript for the JVM and Kotlin`, which is followed directly by the queue items — work them top-to-bottom, honouring the WORK ORDER (its `### Post-v1 backlog` section is parked). (docs/history/PLAN-PHASE-4.md and its earlier `### QUEUE` sections are archived from prior phases — do not work them.) Live session notes also live under that heading, with the most recent at the top. Default order is top-to-bottom, **fixing as many items per session as the budget allows** — do not stop after a single item if there is remaining context and more tractable work ahead. The outer loop:

1. Pick the first unchecked (`- [ ]`) item in the live queue (or next unfinished sub-step of an `IN PROGRESS` item). If the top item appears blocked on missing infrastructure, **do NOT skip it** — instead, promote the unblocker to the top of the queue and work that (see autonomous-decision policy below). The original item stays unchecked and gets picked up automatically once the unblocker lands. Skipping past a blocker is reserved for the narrow user-policy cases listed under "Guardrails."
2. Implement it — the item describes the deliverable. **Every fix ALSO gets hand-written LOCAL tests for its corner cases** (a `*Test.kt` in `src/commonTest/kotlin/`, kotlin.test — user directive 2026-07-02): the generated corpus suite only pins baseline bytes for corpus shapes; the local test pins the fix's INVARIANT beyond them (both associativity directions, nested contexts, off-corpus depths/inputs). Assert the SHARP signal, not just success — e.g. `diagnostics.none { it.code == 2589 }`, because a boundary-guard-masked stack overflow still "passes" (`DeepExpressionChainTest` is the reference; its first run caught 3 latent overflows the corpus depth missed).
3. Run the full suite (`rm -rf build/test-results/jvmTest && ./gradlew jvmTest 2>&1 | grep -a "tests completed"`).
4. Verify no regressions from the currently passing test count.
5. Check off the item (`- [x]`), **bump STATUS.md** (corpus count if it moved, dashboard metrics if the item moved them), add a session note to PLAN-PHASE-5.md (under the live notes heading, most recent on top), add a CLAUDE.md gotcha if applicable, **commit and push** (one commit per sub-step — keeps history bisectable and lets the next agent pick up mid-stream without re-running everything). STATUS.md drift is a real failure mode — past sessions committed `(+N)` work without bumping the count, so the next agent saw a stale baseline. **Trim-on-write (the anti-balloon rule)**: when bumping STATUS.md, keep only the ~5 most-recent round notes — move older ones to docs/history/STATUS-HISTORY.md in the same commit; when adding a session note to PLAN-PHASE-5.md, if more than ~10 rounds are live, move the oldest to docs/history/PLAN-PHASE-5-HISTORY.md in the same commit. Both files ballooned (160 KB / 1.7 MB) before the 2026-06-10 trim because sessions appended without trimming.
6. **Loop back to step 1** and pick up the next item. Keep going until one of these stop conditions:
   - Context budget is running out — finish the current item cleanly, commit, then stop.
   - Every remaining queue item is gated on user-policy decisions (Guardrails) AND no in-scope unblocker work remains. This should be rare — under the promote-unblocker default, blockers turn into queue items, so the queue rarely runs dry.
7. End the session with a concise summary of items completed, unblockers promoted (with what they unblock), items left pending user decisions, and net test count delta.

**Autonomous-decision policy (when an item appears blocked):**

When an item can't be implemented surgically — e.g. the diagnostic relies on infrastructure that doesn't exist yet (super-keyword resolution, full flow-graph, generic argument inference) — **promote the unblocker, do not skip the item**:

- **Promote-unblocker (default)**: identify the smallest concrete infrastructure piece that would unblock the item, insert it at the TOP of the queue with a one-line description and a back-pointer to the original item (and any other items it would also unblock — list them). Then start work on the unblocker. The original item stays unchecked; it gets picked up automatically on a future loop. Cross-phase work (binder + checker together, scope-chain restructuring, new cache layers) is in scope — that is exactly the work this policy exists to drive.
- **Decompose first if large**: if the unblocker is itself a multi-step change, split it into the smallest standalone sub-step that lands as a single commit without regressions, queue that, and only that. Do not attempt the whole infrastructure piece in one pass. Subsequent sub-steps get queued as separate items as the work progresses.
- **Time budget per attempt**: cap each attempt at one full-suite verification cycle. If it regresses, revert. After revert, you may either (a) try a different decomposition for the same unblocker, or (b) move to the next queue item — but record both attempts in the session note. Do not iterate more than twice in one session on the same regressing approach; if neither works, write up what you learned (which paths broke, what the underlying coupling is) and queue a follow-up "investigate X" item.
- **Skip is reserved for Guardrails only**: the only legitimate skip is when the unblocker requires a user-level decision listed under Guardrails below. For those, leave a `BLOCKED-PENDING-USER:` marker on the item with a one-paragraph proposal for the user to react to, then continue with the next queue item. Do NOT end the session.
- **No silent abandonment**: every promote/decompose/skip writes a session note (docs/history/PLAN-PHASE-4.md, most recent on top) with root cause, what was attempted, and what landed. Never leave an item half-done in the working tree.

**Anti-loop rule — recon-only sessions are a protocol failure:** ending a session with no code landed, on the grounds that recon found nothing, is NOT a legitimate outcome while the queue holds unchecked items. In Phase 17 the live pool is the PLAN-PHASE-5.md QUEUE plus any dashboard regression, so "the pool is dry" is never true while unchecked queue items remain; recon is part of step 1, never the deliverable. The full protocol — the `0/0/0` false-exhaustion mechanism, the three-bucket dryness rule, the mis-structured-queue diagnostic, and the forbidden session-end commit patterns — is in the **`corpus-triage` skill**; load it if you are about to end a session without landing code, or when triaging a corpus regression.

**Guardrails requiring user input** (do NOT decide autonomously):
- Build system changes (Gradle config, dependency add/remove/upgrade, Kotlin version bump).
- Test-generation pipeline changes (`generateTypeScriptTests`, baseline format, skipped-tests log structure).
- Re-enabling currently-disabled test categories (e.g. `.errors.txt` → +9k tests).
- EXCEPTIONS pre-approved 2026-07-02 (see PLAN-PHASE-5.md § Approvals): conformance-category adoption in the generator (M3.0), the real-lib migration (M2), and the M5 native-target re-enable.

**Other rules:**
- **Do NOT switch items** mid-task — finish the current item (commit + push) before starting the next.
- **Commit between items** — never bundle two unrelated sub-steps into one commit. Each committed sub-step leaves the repo in a clean state for the next agent.
- **Analysis items** (item 0) should produce written artifacts (design docs, categorized lists) before any code is written.
- **No regressions** — the currently passing tests must continue to pass after every change. Re-run the full suite between sub-steps; a +1 / -2 swap is still a regression.
- **Full-suite run caveat**: a clean JVM test run takes ~4-6 minutes. Budget accordingly when deciding whether to attempt another item.

### Reference TypeScript sources


The original TypeScript compiler source is in `typescript-repo/src/compiler/` (if cloned — only test fixtures are present in the sparse clone). When a fix is ambiguous or behavior is unclear, consult TypeScript's public documentation and source on GitHub. The 1M context window can accommodate large files when needed.

## AI agent workflow


- Keep this file and `PLAN-PHASE-5.md` up to date after each session so the next agent/developer starts with accurate state.
- `PLAN-PHASE-5.md` is the live plan (Phase 17 queue + dashboard + session notes). `docs/history/PLAN-PHASE-4.md` is archived Phase-16 state — its "Known architectural blockers" section is still the reference for the M3 engine items; `docs/history/PLAN-PHASE-4-HISTORY.md` has everything archived — older session notes, completed queue items, the explored-but-skipped SKIP LOG (add new skip entries there, above the `### End of skip log` terminator), the strategic-map archive, gotchas moved out of CLAUDE.md, and the full Phase 5–15 record. `STATUS.md` keeps the headline test count plus the last ~5 round notes; older entries live in `docs/history/STATUS-HISTORY.md`. `docs/history/PLAN-PHASE-3.md` has deferred Phase 3 items (pre-phase-17 artifacts — DESIGN docs, PLAN.md, NEXT-SESSION.md, TYPESCRIPT-TEST-HARNESS.md — also live in `docs/history/`). When grepping for whether a test was previously triaged, grep BOTH `docs/history/PLAN-PHASE-4.md` and `docs/history/PLAN-PHASE-4-HISTORY.md`.
- For subagent-based parallel work (rarely needed — most surgical fixes are faster done in-line), see `docs/subagent-workflow.md`.

## How to run tests


```bash
# Full suite (wipe the WHOLE results dir first — not just /binary; stale XMLs inflate
# the count and survive a /binary-only wipe, see the round-638/690 gotcha above):
rm -rf build/test-results/jvmTest && ./gradlew jvmTest 2>&1 | grep -a "tests completed"

# Single test (get expected vs actual diff):
rm -rf build/test-results/jvmTest && ./gradlew jvmTest --tests '*.<TestName>*' 2>&1 | grep -a -A 40 "message" | head -50
```

**Corpus-regression triage** — the `find_candidates.py` buckets and flags, the skip-log
filtering caveat, the pin-position rules, and the tsgo-relevance policy live in the
**`corpus-triage` skill** (CORPUS-era machinery; load it when diagnosing a corpus-test
regression). Two facts that stay resident because they bite outside triage: all failures
are DETERMINISTIC (run-to-run count variance is a dirty binary cache, never JVM
instability), and **Gradle wipes the XMLs when run with `--tests '*Name*'`** — so the full
suite must be re-run before any XML-reading script can report accurate results.

- **When hand-parsing JUnit XML to enumerate failing tests, use a real XML parser (`xml.etree.ElementTree`), NEVER a regex like `<testcase name="X"[^>]*>(.*?)</testcase>`.** JUnit emits PASSED testcases as self-closing `<testcase .../>` (no body) and FAILED ones with a `<failure>` child. A naive regex consumes a passed test's `/>`, then `(.*?)</testcase>` captures the NEXT (failing) testcase's body and ATTRIBUTES that failure to the passed test — silently shifting names and producing a garbage failing-list. This caused round-174's false "338/364 failures are order-dependent" conclusion (which mis-promoted B450); round-175 re-measured with `xml.etree` and found the 364 failures are GENUINE and DETERMINISTIC (running the failing-base patterns as an isolated batch reproduces all 364). The "Test ordering sensitivity" gotcha above is real but bounded (~25–50 boundary tests, both directions), not the bulk of the failing set.

## Anti-patterns to avoid

- **NEVER PASS `-q` TO THE WARNING GATE — IT SUPPRESSES THE WARNINGS THE GREP IS LOOKING FOR, AND THE RESULT IS A **ZERO-BYTE LOG** THAT READS EXACTLY LIKE "warning-clean" (2026-09-13, measured: a deliberately injected `USELESS_CAST` probe read `bytes=0 w=0` under `--rerun -q` AND under `--rerun-tasks -q`, and `w: … No cast needed.` in 1,364 bytes without it).** Four consecutive rounds recorded a worthless "warning-clean" this way and HEAD was in fact dirty. The documented incantation has no `-q` and never did: `./gradlew compileKotlinJvm compileTestKotlinJvm --rerun-tasks 2>&1 | grep -a '^w:'`. **The general law, which is round 947's with a new costume: an EMPTY log is not evidence of a clean gate — it is evidence of NO OUTPUT, and the two are indistinguishable without a positive control.** Any gate whose pass condition is "nothing printed" must be proven live at least once per session by injecting the thing it is supposed to catch (a throwaway `.kt` with a known warning, deleted afterwards) — and checking the log is non-empty is the cheap half of that.


- Do not add content to this file that is already discoverable by reading the source or build scripts — that inflates context without adding signal, reducing AI agent task success rates (see [arxiv 2602.11988](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.11988)).
- Do not use `grep` (without `-a` flag) on Gradle test output — it may contain binary content. Always use `grep -a`. **SOURCE greps too: `Checker.kt` — and `-project`'s `Project.kt`, measured 2026-08-22 — trip grep's binary heuristic (control bytes in string literals), so a plain `grep pattern src/.../Checker.kt` silently returns NOTHING for text that IS there** — round 500 nearly re-derived `perFileScope` from scratch this way, and an earlier session wrote a stale "never consumed" queue claim the same way. Retry any surprising empty grep over `src/` with `-a` before concluding code is absent.
- **Do not re-analyze what to fix next.** docs/history/PLAN-PHASE-4.md is already the prioritized plan. Pick the top unfinished item, implement it, done. Do not scan lists of failing tests or explore "low-hanging fruits" — that is wasted analysis time that could be implementation time.
